S = T S=T for Shimura Varieties and moduli spaces of p p -adic shtukas
Zhiyou Wu

Abstract
. We prove the S = T S=T conjecture proposed by Xiao–Zhu in [ XZ17 ] , making use of Scholze’s theory of diamonds and v-stacks and Fargues–Scholze’s geometric Satake equivalence. Following [ XZ18 ] , we deduce the Eichler–Shimura relation for Shimura varieties of Hodge type.



1.
Introduction

Xiao–Zhu have raised a question on
S=T
for Shimura varieties in
[
XZ17
, Conjecture 7.3.13]
, which states that excursion operators on the compactly supported cohomology are the same as Hecke operators, and is an analogue for Lafforgue’s
S=T
theorem in
[
Laf18
, Proposition 6.2]
. This is a fundamental question which can be interpreted as a local-global compatibility in a categorical formulation of Langlands correspondence, see
[
Zhu20
, Conjecture 4.7.16]
. Moreover, it has consequences on the Eichler–Shimura relation for compactly supported cohomology of Shimura varieties of Hodge type, see
[
XZ18
, Proposition 6.3.1]
.

The main result of this paper is a proof of this conjecture.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
X_{K}
be a Shimura variety of Hodge type with respect to a reductive group
G
and level
K
. Let
p
be a prime over which
G
is unramified, and
K
is hyperspecial at
p
. Let
l
be a prime different from
p
, then we have an identity

T_{V}=S_{V}

as endormorphisms of

H^{i}_{c}(\mathfrak{X}_{K,\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}},\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
,
where
\mathfrak{X}_{K}
is the smooth integral model of
X_{K}
constructed in
[
Kis10
]
,
V
is a representation of
{}^{L}G
,
T_{V}
is the Hecke operator associated to
V
as defined in section
4
, and
S_{V}
is the excursion operator associated to
V
as defined in
[
XZ17
, Section 7.3.11]
.

An application of such result, as observed by Xiao–Zhu, is the generalization of the classical Eichler–Shimura relation (
[
GS58
]
,
[
Eic54
]
) to Shimura varieties of Hodge type, proving a conjecture of Blasius–Rogawski in this setting.

Corollary 1.2
.

(
[
XZ18
, Proposition 6.3.1]
)
We have the Eichler–Shimura relation for Shimura varieties of Hodge type.

Strictly speaking, there are two versions of
S=T
in mixed characteristics (while there is only one in equal characteristic). The first is
S=T
for moduli spaces of mixed characteristic shtukas, and the second is the one for Shimura varieties. In
[
XZ17
]
, Xiao–Zhu have proved
S=T
for moduli spaces of Witt vector shtukas (
loc.cit.
theorem 6.0.1 (2)), and zero dimensional Shimura varieties (
loc.cit.
proposition 7.3.14). However, even their version for moduli spaces of shtukas is strictly weaker than Lafforgue’s
S=T
theorem in equal characteristics, and is again zero dimensional in nature. More precisely, they have proved that the excursion operator associated to a representation of the Langlands dual group acts on the cohomology of a zero dimensional moduli space of shtukas by the function associated to the representation through classical Satake. While Lafforgue’s
S=T
theorem states that the Hecke correspondence associated to a representation
V
is the same as the excursion operator associated to
V
on any moduli space of shtukas.

The reason Xiao–Zhu only prove their results for zero dimensional objects is the same for both Shimura varieties and moduli spaces of shtukas. Namely, they work with the Witt vector affine Grassmannian and moduli spaces of Witt vector shtukas, which are living over a characteristic
p
base, so they can only talk about the special fiber of Shimura varieties and moduli spaces of shtukas. However, Hecke operators at
p
are only naturally visible (as étale correspondences) in characteristic 0, or at least prime to
p
. The usual treatment is to take the closure of the Hecke correspondence in an integral model and reduce to characteristic
p
, this usually creates a lot of degeneration, as the classical example

T_{p}\equiv\operatorname{Frob}+V

for modular curves shows. This problem only goes away in zero dimension, where the Hecke operators ”flatten” to characteristic
p
. Since Xiao–Zhu can only work with the special fiber, they can not even define Hecke operators for general moduli space of Witt vector shtukas. Indeed, they only state their conjecture and results for cohomology, which is nothing but passing to zero dimension, where they have the obvious notion of Hecke operators, namely the action of a function.

There is a natural way to proceed. Namely the theory of diamonds and v-stacks developed by Scholze in
[
SW20
]
and
[
Sch17
]
allows us to talk about moduli spaces of mixed characteristic shtukas over generic fibers, so we can define Hecke operators for moduli spaces of shtukas. Further, we have integral models of moduli spaces of shtukas using this framework, whose special fiber is essentially the moduli spaces of Witt vector shtukas considered by Xiao–Zhu, and the proof in this article is in some sense a nearby cycle construction relating cohomology in characteristic
0
to cohomology in characteristic
p
.

More precisely, the excursion operators on moduli spaces of shtukas are defined by the composition of creation, partial Frobenius and annihilation correspondences, each of which are essentially pullbacks of cohomological correspondences on Hecke stacks. The key to the construction of these correspondences on the Hecke stacks is the geometric Satake established by Fargues and Scholze in
[
FS21
, Chapter VI]
, which can be viewed as a globalization of the Witt vector geometric Satake established by Zhu in
[
Zhu17
]
. We can perform these constructions on the integral models of moduli spaces of shtukas, whose restriction to the special fiber are the excursion operators for moduli spaces of Witt vector shtukas constructed by Xiao–Zhu.

On the other hand, we can prove that the excursion operators on the generic fiber are the same as the Hecke operators. We follow the same strategy as Vincent Lafforgue’s proof in
[
Laf18
, Proposition 6.2]
, which is basically to move legs to reduce to the zero dimensional case that has already been established by Xiao–Zhu in
[
XZ17
, Proposition 6.0.1 (2)]
.

Now we have the
S=T
for moduli spaces of
p
-adic shtukas, and we want to deduce the corresponding
S=T
for Shimura varieties. Recall that excursion operators for the special fiber of Shimura varieties are defined as the pullback of the excursion operators for moduli spaces of Witt vector shtukas through crystalline period maps from (special fibers of) Shimura varieties to moduli spaces of Witt vector shtukas, see
[
XZ17
, Section 7.2.3]
. We generalize this construction to period maps from integral models of Shimura varieties to (integral) moduli spaces of
p
-adic shtukas. The special fiber of this map is essentially the old crystalline period map, while the generic fiber is closely related to the (quotient of) Hodge–Tate period map in
[
CS17
, Section 2]
or
[
Sch15
, Chapter III.3]
. The construction of this map makes essential use of the classification of
p
-divisible groups over integral perfectoid rings in terms of Breuil–Kisin–Fargues modules in the appendix of
[
SW20
, Lecture 17]
. Now the pullbacks of excursion operators on moduli spaces of
p
-adic shtukas along these period maps produce the excursion operators on Shimura varieties, and the
S=T
for Shimura varieties follows formally from the
S=T
for moduli spaces of shtukas.

The integral period map also appears in the recent work of Georgios Pappas and Michael Rapoport, see
[
PR21
, Section 4]
. Indeed, they construct it more generally for Shimura varieties with parahoric reduction, while we work only with the good reduction case. Moreover, they construct it on the full adic space associated to the integral model of Shimura varieties, while we work only with the good reduction locus.

Under certain restrictive hypothesis, the Eichler–Shimura relation for Shimura varieties of Hodge type has also recently been established by Si Ying Lee in
[
Lee20
]
. We provide an extensive remark on the comparison between the two approaches to congruence relations, which we believe to help clarify the ideas underlying this paper.

The strategy of proof in
[
Lee20
]
is similar to that of Faltings–Chai (
[
FC90
, Chapter VII.4]
) , Torsten Wedhorn (
[
Wed00
]
) and many others. The key to all approaches to congruence relations is to detect the degeneration of
p
-isogenies between abelian varieties from characteristic
0
to characteristic
p
using linear algebra data coming from cohomology theory, and the difference lies in which cohomology theory to use. The approach in
[
FC90
, Chapter VII.4]
is to use
étale cohomology to detect the effect of specialization of isogenies. For the ordinary reduction, this is easy since the specialization just adds a canonical filtration on the Tate module, and the effect on isogenies is simply to preserve this filtration. Hence their proof makes crucial use of ordinary abelian varieties, which restricts the applicability of the method, namely one has to add a condition on the density of the ordinary isogeny locus.

To get rid of this condition, it is necessary to look at the specialization of general abelian varieties and their isogenies, and detect them using linear algebra data. This is exactly what
p
-adic Hodge theory could help us with. In the ordinary case,
p
-adic Hodge theory is hidden since the comparison already takes places at the rational level, namely the canonical filtration. In general, the period rings manifest themselves. However, it is hard to find a direct linear algebra description of the specialization as in the ordinary case. Indeed, it is well-known that the relation between the Hodge–Tate period of a
p
-divisible group over
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
and the Dieudonné module of its special fiber is mysterious. The way we proceed is exactly the way Scholze describes the mysterious relation in
[
SW20
, Lecture 12]

using intermediate integral objects, namely
p
-adic shtukas.

The lack of explicit linear algebra models in the previous approach is remedied by the high power functorial geometric models, which puts us in the framework of geometric Langlands. In particular, the degeneration is described by the comparison theorems in
p
-adic Hodge theory, in the disguise of the Fargues–Fontaine curve (or closely related objects), and the lack of explicit identifications is remedied by the functoriality of Fargues–Fontaine curve, which significantly mechanizes the situation by putting us in the framework of diamonds and v-stacks, so we can mimic what has been done in geometric Langlands. More precisely, the degeneration is reflected by the behaviour of excursion operators when the legs of shtukas collide, somehow the excursion operators ”flatten” the situation by allowing extra freedom on adding legs and intermediate modifications, where the magic ultimately boils down to the fusion product in geometric Satake.

We learned from Scholze after initial progress on this project that Zhu also has a proof of the
S=T
conjecture for some time, which in principle should be similar to the proof presented here.

We now briefly describe the content of this paper. In section 2, we provide definitions for all the spaces that we will use. In section 3,
we recall the geometric Satake proved by Fargues–Scholze, and interpret morphisms between representations of the dual group as cohomological correspondences. In section 4, we introduce the Hecke operators on moduli spaces of shtukas. In section 5,
we construct the excursion operators for moduli spaces of shtukas. In section 6, we prove the
S=T
for moduli spaces of shtukas. In the last section, we construct the integral period maps from Shimura varieties to moduli spaces of shtukas, and prove the
S=T
for Shimura varieties. We collect some results on the comparison between cohomology of algebraic stacks and v-stacks, which is used to compare the constructions given here with the constructions in
[
XZ17
]
.

Convention

We fix a prime
p
throughout. We will work with the theory of v-stacks and diamonds as developed in
[
Sch17
]
, by definition they are stacks over the category of characteristic
p
perfectoid spaces with the v-topology.

We follow the notation in
[
Sch17
, Section 15]
to denote
X^{\diamond}
the diamond associated to an adic space
X
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, which parametrizes untilts
S^{\sharp}
of
S
together with a map
S^{\sharp}\rightarrow X
. When
X=\operatorname{Spa}(A,A^{+})
, we denote as usual

X^{\diamond}=\operatorname{Spd}(A,A^{+})
. When
A^{+}
is clear from the situation, we abbreviate the notation by writing
\operatorname{Spd}(A)=\operatorname{Spd}(A,A^{+})
. For example, when
K
is a non-archimedean field, we write
\operatorname{Spd}(K)=\operatorname{Spd}(K,\mathcal{O}_{K})
.

Let
X
be an algebraic stack or scheme locally of finite type over a discrete valuation ring
\mathcal{O}
with perfect residue field, as in appendix
A.1
, we write
X^{\diamond}
(resp.
X^{\diamond\diamond}
) for the stackification of the prestack sending a characteristic
p
perfectoid space
\operatorname{Spa}(R,R^{+})
to the groupoid of untilts
\operatorname{Spa}(R^{\sharp},R^{\sharp,+})
over
\mathcal{O}
together with a map
\operatorname{Spec}(R^{\sharp,+})\rightarrow X
(resp.
\operatorname{Spec}(R^{\sharp})\rightarrow X
) of algebraic stacks over
\mathcal{O}
. When
X
is a scheme,
this deviates from the notation in
[
Sch17
, Section 27]
, where they denote
X^{\diamond}
for our
X^{\diamond\diamond}
. Our notations of
X^{\diamond}
and
X^{\diamond\diamond}
are different from other authors such as
[
AGLR22
]
, where they use
X^{\diamond}
and
X^{\Diamond}
.

For
E
an extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
,
\operatorname{Spd}(E)
is the diamond parametrizing characteristic 0 untilts with a morphism to
\operatorname{Spa}(E,\mathcal{O}_{E})
, and
\operatorname{Spd}(\mathcal{O}_{E})
is the v-sheaf parametrizing all untilts with a morphism to
\operatorname{Spa}(\mathcal{O}_{E},\mathcal{O}_{E})
.

Following the notation of
[
SW20
, Lecture 13]
, we denote

\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S):=\operatorname{Spa}(W(R^{+}),W(R^{+}))\setminus V([\omega]),

where
S=\operatorname{Spa}(R,R^{+})
is a characteristic
p
affinoid perfectoid space with a topological nilpotent unit
\omega
. For general
S
, we simply glue along affinoid ones.
We have a natural identification

\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)^{\diamond}=S\times\operatorname{Spd}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Then an untilt of
S
is equivalent to a section of

\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)^{\diamond}\rightarrow S
,
giving rise to an effective Cartier divisor on
\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)
.

We denote

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{FF},S}:=\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)/\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{\mathbb{Z}}

the relative Fargues–Fontaine curve with respect to
S
, where

\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S):=\operatorname{Spa}(W(R^{+}),W(R^{+}))\setminus V(p[\omega])

for

S=\operatorname{Spa}(R,R^{+})
. We note that
\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)
is an open subspace of
\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)
, the generic fiber of
\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Moreover, the associated diamond

\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)^{\diamond}

can be identified with

S^{\diamond}\times\operatorname{Spd}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Now let
S^{\sharp}=\operatorname{Spa}(R^{\sharp},R^{\sharp,+})

be an untilt of
S
,
we denote

\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),S^{\sharp}}:=W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{[\omega]}]^{\wedge}_{\xi},

i.e. the
\xi
-adic completion of

W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{[\omega]}]
, where
\xi\in W(R^{+})
is a generator of the kernel of
W(R^{+})\rightarrow R^{\sharp,+}
, and
\omega\in R^{+}
is a pseudo-uniformizer. Note that

\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),S^{\sharp}}=\mathbb{B}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}(R^{\sharp})

if
R^{\sharp}
is of characteristic
0
, and

\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),S^{\sharp}}=W(R)

if
S^{\sharp}=S
, where
\mathbb{B}_{\operatorname{dR}}^{+}(R^{\sharp})

is the
\xi
-adic completion of
W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{p}]

with
\xi\in W(R^{+})
being a generator of the kernel of the canonical map
W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{p}]\rightarrow R^{\sharp}
.

When we have finitely many untilts
S_{i}^{\sharp}=\operatorname{Spa}(R^{\sharp}_{i},R^{\sharp,+}_{i})
of
S
(possibly not all distinct), we denote

\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),\sum S_{i}^{\sharp}}:=W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{[\omega]}]^{\wedge}_{\xi},

where
\xi=\prod\xi_{i}
with
\xi_{i}
a generator of the kernel of
W(R^{+})\rightarrow R_{i}^{\sharp,+}
.

We will use frequently the formal properties of cohomological correspondences. Let

X,Y
and
Z
be small v-stacks which form a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

where we assume that
t
is compactifiable, representable in locally spatial diamonds with locally dim.trg
<\infty
.
Let
\Lambda=\mathcal{O}_{K}
with
K
a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
,
A\in D_{\text{ét}}(X,\Lambda)
and
B\in D_{\text{ét}}(Y,\Lambda)
, then
a cohomological correspondence
from

A
to
B

supported on
Z
, denoted by

\mathscr{C}:(X,A)\longrightarrow(Y,B),

is defined to be a morphism

\mathscr{C}:s^{*}A\longrightarrow t^{!}B

in
D_{\text{ét}}(Z,\Lambda)
.

Let us be given a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

of small
v
-stacks. Let
\mathscr{C}:(X,A)\rightarrow(Y,B)
be a cohomological correspondence supported on
Z
, then we can pullback
\mathscr{C}
along the diagram
to obtain a cohomological correspondence

f^{*}\mathscr{C}:(\mathcal{X},h^{*}A)\longrightarrow(\mathcal{Y},g^{*}B)

supported on
\mathcal{Z}
,
if either the right square of the diagram is Cartesian or
f,g
and
h
are cohomologically smooth, see
[
XZ17
, Appendix A.2.11]
.

On the other hand, let
\mathscr{C}:(\mathcal{X},A)\rightarrow(\mathcal{Y},B)
be a cohomological correspondence supported on
\mathcal{Z}
, and suppose that
s^{\prime}
and
s
are proper, then we can !-pushforward
\mathscr{C}
to obtain a cohomological correspondence

Rf_{!}\mathscr{C}:(X,Rh_{!}A)\longrightarrow(Y,Rg_{!}B)

supported on
Z
, see
[
XZ17
, Appendix A.2.6]
.

Moreover, we can compose cohomological correspondences, and pullback and !-pushforward commute with composition, see appendix A.2 of
loc.cit.
. The cohomological correspondences and their relation with 6-functor formalism are studied systematically in the more recent works, see
[
HM24
]
and
[
LZ22
]
.

2.
Basic Definitions

Let
G
be a reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, together with a reductive model
\mathcal{G}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Then
G
is unramified, so split over
\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}:=\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[\frac{1}{p}]
, where
\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}:=W(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})
. Let

\mu
be a conjugacy class of cocharacters

\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
,
whose field of definition is
E
, which is contained in
\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
(by the unramifiedness of
G
).

Let us first introduce notations that we will use frequently. Let
S
be an affinoid perfectoid space of characteristic
p
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}

\mathcal{G}
-torsors on

\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)
, and
S^{\sharp}
an untilt of
S
. We will use the dotted arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

to denote a modification from
\mathcal{P}_{1}
to
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, i.e.
\varphi
is an isomorphism

\varphi:\mathcal{P}_{1}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus S^{\sharp}}\cong\mathcal{P}_{2}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus S^{\sharp}}

that is meromorphic at
S^{\sharp}
, in the sense of
[
SW20
, Definition 5.3.5]
. We can also have modifications with respect to finitely many untilts of
S
.

Let
\mu
be a dominant cocharacter of
G
, we will say that
\varphi
is bounded by
\mu
at
S^{\sharp}

if for any geometric rank 1 point
C
of
S
, and trivializations of

\mathcal{P}_{1}

and
\mathcal{P}_{2}

over

\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(C),C^{\sharp}}[\frac{1}{\xi}]
, the ”punctured formal neighborhood of
C^{\sharp}
”,

\varphi

lies in

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(C),C^{\sharp}})\lambda(\xi)\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(C),C^{\sharp}})

for some
\lambda\leq\mu
,
where

\xi\in\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(C),C^{\sharp}}

is a uniformizer, and

\lambda(\xi)\in G(\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(C),C^{\sharp}}[\frac{1}{\xi}])

is the image of
\xi
under (
\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(C),C^{\sharp}}[\frac{1}{\xi}]
-points of)
\lambda
. Note that this is a pointwise condition on
S
.

The definitions introduced below are only prestacks a priori, but it follows directly
from
[
SW20
, Proposition 19.5.3]
and
[
SW20
, Corollary 17.1.9]
that they are all stacks, with the exception on (global) Witt vector shtukas and Hecke stacks in section
2.3
, where the stackification is necessary.

2.1.
Hecke stacks

We first introduce Hecke stacks.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
I
be a finite set, and
\mu_{\bullet}
be a collection of dominant cocharacters of
G
indexed by
I
. Let
\{I_{1},\cdots,I_{k}\}
be a partition of
I
. We define

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

to be the stack over
\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I}
(
:=\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})}\cdots\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
) whose value at
S
is the groupoid of

\bullet

\mathcal{G}
-torsors
\mathcal{P}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{P}_{k+1}
on

\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)

\bullet

untilts

S^{\sharp}_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)

of
S
defined over
\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
indexed by
i\in I
, and

\bullet

modifications

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

where the dotted arrows
\varphi_{i}
are isomorphisms

\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{P}_{i}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{j\in I_{i}}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{j}}\cong\mathcal{P}_{i+1}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{j\in I_{i}}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{j}}

that are meromorphic along the Cartier divisor

\underset{j\in I_{i}}{\sum}S^{\sharp}_{j}
of

\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)
.

We define

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

to be the closed substack of
\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

parametrizing the data as

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
does with the additional condition that
at every geometric rank 1 point
C
of
S
and untilt
C^{\sharp}
of
C
,

\varphi_{i}
is bounded by

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in I_{i}\\
C^{\sharp}\in S^{\sharp}_{j}\end{subarray}}{\sum}\mu_{j}

at
C^{\sharp}
, where
C^{\sharp}\in S^{\sharp}_{j}
means that the canonical untilt (over
S_{j}^{\sharp}
) of
C
over
S
, under the equivalence between perfectoid spaces over
S^{\sharp}_{j}
and perfectoid spaces over
S
, is identified with
C^{\sharp}
.

When the partition is trivial, we make the following simplification

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}:=\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I)},

which parametrizes

\varphi:\mathcal{P}_{1}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{i\in I}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{i}}\cong\mathcal{P}_{2}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{i\in I}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{i}}

that are meromorphic and bounded by
\mu_{i}
at each untilt
S_{i}^{\sharp}
of
S
.

Remark 2.2
.

We note that
\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{\mu_{\bullet}}

is really defined over the product of ring of integers of the defining fields of
\mu_{\bullet}
. The base can be further refined if we do not specify the boundedness condition. Indeed,
\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
can be naturally defined over
\operatorname{Spd}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{I}
, i.e. it is the base change to
\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I}
of a v-stack defined over
\operatorname{Spd}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{I}
.

We observe that there are natural maps between Hecke stacks.

Let

\zeta:I\rightarrow J

be a map of finite sets,
(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})
be a partition of
J
, and
I_{i}=\zeta^{-1}J_{i}
, there exists a natural map

(1)

i_{\zeta}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})},

where we abuse the notation by writing
\zeta:\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I}

the permutation map corresponding to
\zeta
, i.e. sending
(x_{j})_{j\in J}
to
(x_{\zeta(i)})_{i\in I}
. It is defined by sending

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

to itself, except we just relabel the legs using
\zeta
. More precisely, it sends

\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{P}_{i}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{k\in I_{i}}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{\zeta(k)}}\cong\mathcal{P}_{i+1}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{k\in I_{i}}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{\zeta(k)}}

to

\varphi_{i}^{\prime}:\mathcal{P}_{i}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{j\in J_{i}}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{j}}\cong\mathcal{P}_{i+1}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{j\in J_{i}}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{j}},

which is the restriction of
\varphi_{i}
to

\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{j\in J_{i}}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{j}
, i.e.
the modification at
S^{\sharp}_{j}
is given by
\varphi_{i}
if
j\in\zeta(I_{i})
, and it is trivial if
j\notin\zeta(I_{i})
.

Moreover, we have the convolution map

(2)

\pi^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I)},

which is defined by sending

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}\cdots\varphi_{1}}

Lastly, we have the maps that split the partition.
Let
(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})
be a partition of
I
such that
J_{j}=I_{i_{j}}\cup\cdots\cup I_{i_{j+1}-1}
for some partition
1=i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{l+1}=k+1
of
k+1
. We define

(3)

\kappa^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\longrightarrow\underset{j}{\prod}\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{i_{j}},\cdots,I_{i_{j+1}-1})}

to be the forgetting map that sends

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

to

\underset{j}{\prod}(\hbox to291.85pt{\vbox to20.73pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 145.92203pt\lower-8.89583pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Now we introduce the correspondence version for Hecke stacks.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
I,J
be finite sets, and
\mu_{\bullet},\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}
be collections of dominant cocharacters of
G
indexed by
I
and
J
respectively. Let
\nu
be a dominant cocharacter of
G
. Then we define

\operatorname{Hecke}^{\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}

to be the stack with value at a characteristic
p
perfectoid space
S
being the groupoid of the data of

\bullet

\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2},\varphi\}\in\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}(S)

and

\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}\}\in\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}}(S)
,

\bullet
(meromorphic) modifications bounded by
\nu

\gamma_{1}:\mathcal{P}_{1}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)}\cong\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)}

\gamma_{2}:\mathcal{P}_{2}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)}\cong\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{2}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)}

at the characteristic
p
untilt

S\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)

such that the following diagram is commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}

We will occasionally use the following more general version.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
I,J
be finite sets, and
\mu_{\bullet},\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}
be collections of dominant cocharacters of
G
indexed by
I
and
J
respectively. Let
\{I_{1},\cdots,I_{k}\}
and
\{J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}\}
be partitions of
I
and
J
. Let
\nu
be a dominant cocharacter of
G
. Then we define

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}

to be the stack with value at a characteristic
p
perfectoid space
S
being the groupoid of the data of

\bullet

\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{P}_{k+1},\varphi_{1},\cdots\varphi_{k}\}\in\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}(S)

and

\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{P}_{l+1}^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\cdots\varphi_{l}^{\prime}\}\in\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}(S)
,

\bullet
modifications bounded by
\nu
(in the sense similar to the above)

\gamma_{1}:\mathcal{P}_{1}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)}\cong\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)}

\gamma_{2}:\mathcal{P}_{k+1}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)}\cong\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{l+1}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)}

at the characteristic
p
untilt

S\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)

such that the following diagram is commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{l+1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{l-1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{l}^{\prime}}

We will also make use of a local version of Hecke stacks, which parametrizes modifications of torsors over the formal neighborhoods of Cartier divisors corresponding to untilts.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
I
be a finite set, and
\mu_{\bullet}
be a collection of dominant cocharacters of
G
indexed by
I
. Let
\{I_{1},\cdots,I_{k}\}
be a partition of
I
. We define

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

to be the stack over
\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I}
whose value at
S
is the groupoid of

\bullet

untilts

S^{\sharp}_{i}

of
S
defined over
\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
indexed by
i\in I
,

\bullet

\mathcal{G}
-torsors
\mathcal{P}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{P}_{k+1}
on

Spec(\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),\sum S_{i}^{\sharp}})
, and,

\bullet

modifications

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

where the dotted arrows
\varphi_{i}
are isomorphisms

\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{P}_{i}|_{\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),\sum S_{k}^{\sharp}}[\frac{1}{\underset{j\in I_{i}}{\prod}\xi_{j}}]}\cong\mathcal{P}_{i+1}|_{\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),\sum S_{k}^{\sharp}}[\frac{1}{\underset{j\in I_{i}}{\prod}\xi_{j}}]}

such that at every geometric rank 1 point
C
of
S
and untilt
C^{\sharp}
of
C
,

\varphi_{i}
is bounded by

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in I_{i}\\
C^{\sharp}\in S^{\sharp}_{j}\end{subarray}}{\sum}\mu_{j}

at
C^{\sharp}
.

When the partition is trivial, we make the following simplification

\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\mu_{\bullet}}:=\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{{\operatorname{loc}},(I)}.

Lastly, we denote

\operatorname{Hecke}^{{\operatorname{loc}},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:=\underset{\mu_{\bullet}}{\bigcup}\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{{\operatorname{loc}},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}.

Remark 2.6
.

When the partition of
I
is trivial, the local Hecke stack is defined in
[
FS21
, Definition VI.1.6]
. The general partition case also appears in the proof of
[
FS21
, Definition/proposition VI.9.4]
.

Remark 2.7
.

The drawback of local Hecke stacks is that there is no obvious correspondence between them, due to that the torsors are defined on different domains. However, this problem disappears if we fix the legs at characteristic
p
untilts. An important example is the Witt vector local Hecke stacks to be defined below, where we have the correspondences.

There is a canonical map

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

being the restriction from global
G
-torsors to local
G
-torsors. We will use it to define the Satake sheaves on
\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
.

Let
L^{+}\mathcal{G}
be the group v-sheaf over
\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})

whose value at
S
is

L^{+}\mathcal{G}(S)=\{(x,S^{\sharp})|S^{\sharp}\in\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})(S),x\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),S^{\sharp}})\},

and

Gr_{\mathcal{G},\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),\leq\mu}

the Beilinson–Drinfeld affine Grassmannian as defined in
[
SW20
, Definition 20.3.1]
, which classifies
\mathcal{G}
-torsors on the formal neighborhood of
S^{\sharp}
together with a trivialization of the generic fiber that is bounded by
\mu
. By
[
SW20
, Proposition 20.5.4]
,

Gr_{\mathcal{G},\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),\leq\mu}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})

is representable in spatial diamonds, and so in particular a small v-sheaf. By definition, we have

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu}^{{\operatorname{loc}}}=L^{+}\mathcal{G}\setminus Gr_{\mathcal{G},\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),\leq\mu}

as a stack quotient,
where
L^{+}\mathcal{G}
acts on the trivialization, so

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu}^{{\operatorname{loc}}}
is a small v-stack.

We have similar expression for the global Hecke stack
\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu}
, which can be written as the quotient of

Gr_{\mathcal{G},\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),\leq\mu}

by the group v-sheaf parametrizing automorphisms of the trivial global
\mathcal{G}
-torsors on
\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}
. Moreover, for Hecke stacks with multiple legs, it can be written again as quotients of twisted products of affine Grassmannian, see the beginning of
[
FS21
, Chapter VI]
(for the version of local Hecke stacks).

If the characteristic of the untilt
S^{\sharp}
is
p
, then
S^{\sharp}=S
, and

\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),S^{\sharp}}=W(R)

if
S=\operatorname{Spa}(R,R^{+})
. Then the fiber of the Beilinson–Drinfeld affine Grassmannian at the characteristic
p
untilt is the Witt vector affine Grassmannian considered in
[
Zhu17
]
.
1
1

1

We can embed perfect schemes into the category of v-sheaves using either the
\diamond
- or
\diamond\diamond
-analytification functor as explained in appendix
A.1
. The Witt vector affine Grassmannian is ind-proper, so the two
v
-sheaves associated to it are the same.

On the other hand, if

S^{\sharp}=\operatorname{Spa}(R^{\sharp},R^{\sharp,+})

is a characteristic zero untilt, we have

\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),S^{\sharp}}=\mathbb{B}^{+}_{\text{dR}}(R^{\sharp}),

and the fiber of

Gr_{\mathcal{G},\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),\leq\mu}

at
S^{\sharp}
is the

\mathbb{B}^{+}_{\text{dR}}
-affine Grassmannian as defined in
[
SW20
, Lecture 19]
.

Similar to global Hecke stacks, there are natural maps between local Hecke stacks.

Let

\zeta:I\rightarrow J

be a map of finite sets,
(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})
be a partition of
J
, and
I_{i}=\zeta^{-1}J_{i}
, there exists a natural map

(4)

i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})},

where we abuse the notation by writing
\zeta:\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I}

the permutation map corresponding to
\zeta
, i.e. sending
(x_{j})_{j\in J}
to
(x_{\zeta(i)})_{i\in I}
. It is defined by sending

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

to itself, except we just relabel the legs using
\zeta
. More precisely, it sends

\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{P}_{i}|_{\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),\sum S_{k}^{\sharp}}[\frac{1}{\underset{h\in I_{i}}{\prod}\xi_{\zeta(h)}}]}\cong\mathcal{P}_{i+1}|_{\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),\sum S_{k}^{\sharp}}[\frac{1}{\underset{h\in I_{i}}{\prod}\xi_{\zeta(h)}}]}

to

\varphi_{i}^{\prime}:\mathcal{P}_{i}|_{\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),\sum S_{k}^{\sharp}}[\frac{1}{\underset{j\in J_{i}}{\prod}\xi_{j}}]}\cong\mathcal{P}_{i+1}|_{\mathcal{O}^{\land}_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S),\sum S_{k}^{\sharp}}[\frac{1}{\underset{j\in J_{i}}{\prod}\xi_{j}}]},

where the modification at
S^{\sharp}_{j}
is given by
\varphi_{i}
if
j\in\zeta(I_{i})
, and it is trivial, i.e. no modification at all, if
j\notin\zeta(I_{i})
.

Moreover, we have the convolution map

(5)

\pi^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)},

which is defined by sending

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}\cdots\varphi_{1}}

Lastly, we have the maps that split the partition.
Let
(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})
be a partition of
I
such that
J_{j}=I_{i_{j}}\cup\cdots\cup I_{i_{j+1}-1}
for some partition
1=i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{l+1}=k+1
of
k+1
. We define

(6)

\kappa^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\longrightarrow\underset{j}{\prod}\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{i_{j}},\cdots,I_{i_{j+1}-1})}

to be the forgetting map that sends

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

to

\underset{j}{\prod}(\hbox to291.85pt{\vbox to20.73pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 145.92203pt\lower-8.89583pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Notation 2.8
.

Throughout the paper, we use the following notation to denote the restriction to the generic fiber,

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet},\eta_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:=\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})^{I}}

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet},\eta_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:=\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})^{I}}

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime},\eta_{\bullet}|\eta_{\bullet}}:=\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})^{I}\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})^{J}}.

Similarly for the restriction to the special fiber,

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet},s_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:=\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})^{I}}

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet},s_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:=\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})^{I}}

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime},s_{\bullet}|s_{\bullet}}:=\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})^{I}\times\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})^{J}}.

We also have the mixed version, for example

\operatorname{Hecke}_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}),s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\})}:=\operatorname{Hecke}^{(\{1\},\{2\})}_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})}.

2.2.
Moduli spaces of
p
-adic shtukas

We now introduce the critical objects of study in this article, the moduli spaces of shtukas.

Definition 2.9
.

The moduli space of shtukas associated to
\mathcal{G}
and
\mu

is the stack

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu}

over
\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
whose value at a characteristic
p
perfectoid space
S
is the groupoid of the data of

\bullet
a
\mathcal{G}
-torsor
\mathcal{P}
over
\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)
,

\bullet
an (not necessarily of characteristic 0) untilt

S^{\sharp}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)

of
S
defined over
\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, and

\bullet
an isomorphism

\varphi_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{P}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus S^{\sharp}}\cong\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus S^{\sharp}}.

that is meromorphic along the Cartier divisor

S^{\sharp}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)
,
and bounded by
\mu
.

Remark 2.10
.

Although we work with the base
\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, the definition of moduli spaces of shtukas really depends on the group
\mathcal{G}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
rather than its base change to
\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, as otherwise the Frobenius pullback
\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}
does not make sense (it will be a torsor with respect to a Frobenius twist of the structure group over
\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
).

Remark 2.11
.

The definition given here is close to the classical function field counterpart, which is slightly different from the ones in
[
SW20
, Definition 25.1.1 and 23.3.1]
in that we do not fix a trivialization of the
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-torsor as in
loc.cit.
, nor do we fix the type of shtukas in
B(G)
. The treatment in
loc.cit.
is to emphasize the way moduli spaces of shtukas generalize Rapoport--Zink spaces
2
2

2

Indeed, the classical Rapoport Zink space can be identified with the fiber at
b
of a canonical map
\operatorname{Sht_{\mu}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Bun}_{G}
.
, whereas we want to mimic the treatment of Lafforgue in
[
Laf18
]
.

We will also make use of moduli spaces of shtukas with several legs.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
I
be a finite set, and
\mu_{\bullet}
be a collection of dominant cocharacters of
G
indexed by
I
. Let
\{I_{1},\cdots,I_{k}\}
be a partition of
I
.
The moduli space of shtukas associated to
G
,
\mu_{\bullet}
and
\{I_{1},\cdots,I_{k}\}

is the stack

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

over
\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I}
whose value at a characteristic
p
perfectoid space
S
is the groupoid of the data of

\bullet

\mathcal{G}
-torsors
\mathcal{P}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{P}_{k}
over
\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)
,

\bullet
(not necessarily of characteristic 0) untilts

S^{\sharp}_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)

of
S
defined over
\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
indexed by
i\in I
, and

\bullet
modifications

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

where the dotted arrows
\varphi_{i}
are isomorphisms

\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{P}_{i}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{j\in I_{i}}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{j}}\cong\mathcal{P}_{i+1}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{j\in I_{i}}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{j}}

with
\mathcal{P}_{k+1}=\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}_{1}
, which are meromorphic along the Cartier divisor

\underset{j\in I_{i}}{\sum}S^{\sharp}_{j}
of

\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)
.
Moreover, at every geometric rank 1 point
C
of
S
and untilt
C^{\sharp}
of
C
,

\varphi_{i}
is bounded by

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in I_{i}\\
C^{\sharp}\in S^{\sharp}_{j}\end{subarray}}{\sum}\mu_{j}

at
C^{\sharp}
.

When the partition is trivial, we drop the upper script to simplify the notation, i.e.

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}:=\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I)},

which parametrizes isomorphisms of
\mathcal{G}
-torsors

\mathcal{P}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{i\in I}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{i}}\cong\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)\setminus\underset{i\in I}{\cup}S^{\sharp}_{i}}

that are meromorphic and bounded by
\mu_{i}
at each untilt
S_{i}^{\sharp}
of
S
.

The following variant defines a correspondence between moduli spaces of shtukas, and will be very important for our purpose.

Definition 2.13
.

Let

\mu,\mu^{\prime}
and
\nu
be conjugacy classes of cocharacters

\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
. We define

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu|\mu^{\prime}}

to be the stack over
\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{2}
with value at a characteristic
p
perfectoid space
S
being the groupoid of the data of

\bullet

\mathcal{P}\in\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu}(S)
and

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu^{\prime}}(S)
,

\bullet
a meromorphic modification

\gamma:\mathcal{P}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)}\cong\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{(0,\infty)}(S)}

at the characteristic
p
untilt

S\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)

as shtukas, i.e. the following diagram is commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}}

with the notation that dashed arrows denote generically defined modifications.

We define

\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu}_{\mu|\mu^{\prime}}

to be the closed substack of

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu|\mu^{\prime}}

parametrizing the data as
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu|\mu^{\prime}}
does with the additional condition that
\gamma
is bounded by
\nu
.

Similarly, we have the version for moduli spaces of shtukas with several legs.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
I,J
be finite sets, and
\mu_{\bullet},\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}
be collections of dominant cocharacters of
G
indexed by
I
and
J
respectively. Let
\{I_{1},\cdots,I_{k}\}

and

. Then we define

to be the stack with value at a characteristic

being the groupoid of the data of

\bullet

and

\{\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\prime},\varphi_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,\varphi_{l}^{\prime}\}\in\operatorname{Sht}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}_{\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}}(S)
,

\bullet
a modification bounded by

(in the sense similar to the above)

at the characteristic

untilt

S\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)

as shtukas, i.e. the following diagram is commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{l}^{\prime}}

When the partitions of
I
and
J
are trivial, we will simplify the notation by writing

\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}:=\operatorname{Sht}^{(I)|(J),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}},

which parametrizes commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}}

where
\varphi_{\mathcal{P}}
(resp.

) is meromorphic and bounded by
\sum_{i}\mu_{i}
(resp.

) at each untilt

of

, where

(resp.

(resp.

) for which

(resp.

).

Remark 2.15
.

Let
E_{i}
be the reflex field of
\mu_{i}
, then
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
is actually defined over
\prod\operatorname{Spd}(\mathcal{O}_{E_{i}})
, and similarly for
\operatorname{Sht}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}
. The same refinement works for all the spaces defined in this section.

Notation 2.16
.

We will use similar notations as in Notation
2.8
for the restriction of moduli spaces of shtukas to the generic fiber,

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet},\eta_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:=\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})^{I}},

\operatorname{Sht}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet},\eta_{\bullet}|\eta_{\bullet}}:=\operatorname{Sht}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})^{I}\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})^{J}},

and in particular

\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu}_{\mu|\mu^{\prime},\eta_{\bullet}|\eta_{\bullet}}:=\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu}_{\mu|\mu^{\prime}}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})}.

Note that in the last case,
\mu
has to equal to
\mu^{\prime}
.

Similarly, we have the restriction to the special fiber

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet},s_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:=\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})^{I}},

\operatorname{Sht}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet},s_{\bullet}|s_{\bullet}}:=\operatorname{Sht}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})^{I}\times\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})^{J}}.

We also have the mixed version, for example

\operatorname{Sht}_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}),s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\})}:=\operatorname{Sht}^{(\{1\},\{2\})}_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})}.

We have the following alternative characterization of moduli spaces of shtukas over the generic fiber.

Theorem 2.17
.

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu,\eta}
is equivalent to the stack over
\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
sending a characteristic
p
perfectoid space
S
over
\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
to the groupoid of

\bullet

G
-torsors
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
\mathcal{E}
over the Fargues–Fontaine curve

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{FF},S}

such that
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is trivial at every geometric point of
S
. The data
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is equivalent to a pro-étale
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-torsor
\mathbb{L}_{\eta}
on
S
by
[
KL15
, Theorem 8.5.5]
,

\bullet

an untilt

S^{\sharp}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{[0,\infty)}(S)

of
S
defined over
\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
,

\bullet

an isomorphism

\alpha:\mathcal{E}_{0}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{FF},S}\setminus S^{\sharp}}\cong\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{FF},S}\setminus S^{\sharp}}

that is meromorphic along
S^{\sharp}
and bounded by
\mu
,

\bullet

a sub
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-torsor
\mathbb{L}
inside
\mathbb{L}_{\eta}
.

Proof.

The proof of
[
SW20
, Proposition 23.3.1]
goes without change. Indeed, in
loc.cit.
they fix the type of shtukas in
\operatorname{B}(G)
, which corresponds to rigidifying
\mathcal{E}
. Dropping this extra condition gives exactly the data as in the statement.
∎

We have a natural correspondence diagram

(7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu,\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu,\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}

where
p_{1}
(resp.
p_{2}
) sends
\{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\gamma,S^{\sharp}\}

to
\{\mathcal{P},S^{\sharp}\}
(resp.
\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime},S^{\sharp}\}
).

Proposition 2.18
.

The correspondence (
7
) is an étale correspondence, i.e. both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are finite étale morphisms.

Proof.

With the characterization of theorem
2.17
, we see that
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}^{\nu}(S)
parametrizes

\bullet

\{\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{E},\alpha,\mathbb{L},S^{\sharp}\}\in\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu,\eta}(S)
,

\bullet

\{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{0},\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\mathbb{L}^{\prime},S^{\sharp}\}\in\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu,\eta}(S)
,

\bullet

isomorphisms

\beta_{0}:\mathcal{E}_{0}\cong\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{0}

\beta:\mathcal{E}\cong\mathcal{E}^{\prime}

such that

\alpha^{\prime}\circ\beta_{0}=\beta\circ\alpha
, and
under the isomorphism

\mathbb{L}_{\eta}\cong\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{\eta}

of pro-étale
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-torsors on
S
induced by
\beta_{0}
, the sub-
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-torsors
\mathbb{L}
and
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
have relative position bounded by
\nu
at every rank 1 geometric point of
S
.

Now for a given
S
-point of
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu,\eta}
specified by the data
\{\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{E},\alpha,\mathbb{L},S^{\sharp}\}\in\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu,\eta}(S)
, we see that the fiber of
p_{1}
at this point is the data of
sub-
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-torsors
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}

of
\mathbb{L}_{\eta}
which have relative position with respect to
\mathbb{L}
bounded by
\nu
at every geometric point of
S
. Since pro-étale locally
\mathbb{L}_{\eta}
is the trivial torsor
S\times G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, the relevant sub-
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-torsors correspond to a finite subset of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})/\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
determined by
\nu

3
3

3

It is the
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-points of the proper Witt vector Schubert variety with respect to
\mathcal{G}
and
\nu
.
,
which is visibly finite étale over
S
. Thus we see that
p_{1}
is finite étale after base change to a pro-étale cover, hence it is finite étale by
[
Sch17
, Corollary 9.11 (iii)]
.

The same argument shows that
p_{2}
is finite étale.
∎

2.3.
Moduli spaces of Witt vector shtukas and Witt vector Hecke stacks

We will need the Witt vector version of moduli spaces of shtukas and Hecke stacks as well.

Definition 2.19
.

Let
I
be a finite set, and
\mu_{\bullet}
be a collection of dominant cocharacters of
G
indexed by
I
.
The moduli space of Witt vector shtukas over

\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})
associated to
G
and
\mu_{\bullet}
, denoted by

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{W},

is the stackification of the prestack sending a characteristic
p
affinoid perfectoid space
S=Spa(R,R^{+})
over
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
to the groupoid of the data of

\bullet

\mathcal{G}
-torsors
\mathcal{P}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{P}_{k}
over
\operatorname{Spec}(W(R^{+}))
, and

\bullet
modifications

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

where the dotted arrows
\varphi_{i}
are isomorphisms

\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{P}_{i}|_{\operatorname{Spec}(W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{p}])}\cong\mathcal{P}_{i+1}|_{\operatorname{Spec}(W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{p}])}

with
\mathcal{P}_{k+1}=\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}_{1}
, which are bounded by
\mu_{i}
at every geometric point of
S
.

We have the local version as well, which is already a stack by
[
SW20
, Corollary 17.1.9]
.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
I
be a finite set, and
\mu_{\bullet}
be a collection of dominant cocharacters of

.

with

, which are bounded by

at every geometric point of

.

The possibility of having such a definition comes from the fact that Frobenius fixes the characteristic
p
legs, which is not the case for the characteristic 0 ones.

Remark 2.21
.

Note that there is no extra data on the partition of
I
since we fix legs at the characteristic
p
untilt, so essentially the only possibility of the partition is
(\{1\},\cdots,\{k\})
. For example, moduli spaces of Witt vector shtukas with respect to
(\{1,2\},\{3\})
and cocharacters
\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3}\}
is the same as moduli spaces of Witt vector shtukas with respect to

(\{1\},\{2\})
and cocharacters
\{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2},\mu_{3}\}
.

Again, there is the correspondence version.

Definition 2.22
.

Let

be finite sets, and
\mu_{\bullet},\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}

and

,

a modification bounded by

as shtukas, i.e. the following diagram is commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\prime}.}

We now define the Witt vector Hecke stacks.

Definition 2.23
.

Let
I
be a finite set, and
\mu_{\bullet}
be a collection of dominant cocharacters of
G
indexed by
I
. We define

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{W}

to be the stackification of the prestack sending
S=Spa(R,R^{+})
over
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
to the groupoid of

\bullet

\mathcal{G}
-torsors
\mathcal{P}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{P}_{k+1}
on

Spec(W(R^{+}))
,

\bullet

modifications

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

where the dotted arrows
\varphi_{i}
are isomorphisms

\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{P}_{i}|_{\operatorname{Spec}(W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{p}])}\cong\mathcal{P}_{i+1}|_{\operatorname{Spec}(W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{p}])}

that are bounded by
\mu_{i}
at every geometric point of
S
.

Remark 2.24
.

One can also define a local version of the Witt vector Hecke stack, but this coincides with the special fiber of
\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\mu_{\bullet}}
.

Definition 2.25
.

Let
I,J
be finite sets, and
\mu_{\bullet},\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}
be collections of dominant cocharacters of
G
indexed by
I
and
J
respectively. Let
\nu
be a dominant cocharacter of
G
. Then we define

\operatorname{Hecke}^{\nu,W}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}

to be the stackification of the prestack sending a characteristic
p
affinoid perfectoid space
S=Spa(R,R^{+})
over
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
to the groupoid of the data of

\bullet

\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{P}_{k+1},\varphi_{1},\cdots\varphi_{k}\}\in\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{W}(S)

and

\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{P}_{l+1}^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\cdots\varphi_{l}^{\prime}\}\in\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}}^{W}(S)
,

\bullet
