\nu

\gamma_{1}:\mathcal{P}_{1}|_{\operatorname{Spec}(W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{p}])}\cong\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1}|_{\operatorname{Spec}(W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{p}])}

\gamma_{2}:\mathcal{P}_{k+1}|_{\operatorname{Spec}(W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{p}])}\cong\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{l+1}|_{\operatorname{Spec}(W(R^{+})[\frac{1}{p}])}

such that the following diagram is commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{l+1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{l-1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{l}^{\prime}}

We observe that the moduli spaces of Witt vector shtukas and Hecke stacks are analytifications of perfect stacks. Let
\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc}(\infty)}_{\mu_{\bullet}}
be the perfect Hecke stack as defined in
[
XZ17
, Definition 5.1.1]
, see also appendix
A.3
, then it is easy to see that

\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc}(\infty),\diamond}_{\mu_{\bullet}}=\operatorname{Hecke}^{W}_{\mu_{\bullet}}

and

\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc}(\infty),\diamond\diamond}_{\mu_{\bullet}}=\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\cdots,\{n\})}_{\mu_{\bullet},s_{\bullet}}

with notations as in
A.1
. On the other hand, we have

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc}(\infty),\diamond}=\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{W}

and

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc}(\infty),\diamond\diamond}=\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc},W}

where

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc}(\infty)}

is the untruncated moduli space of Witt vector shtukas defined in
[
XZ17
, Definition 5.2.1]
. Similarly for the correspondence version
\operatorname{Hecke}^{\nu,W}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}

and

\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu,W}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}
.

As a sanity check, all the stacks defined above are small v-stacks.

Proposition 2.26
.

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
,

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
,

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}
,
\operatorname{Hecke}^{W}_{\mu_{\bullet}}
,

\operatorname{Hecke}^{\nu,W}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}
,

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
,

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc},W}
,

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{W}
,

\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu,W}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}

and
\operatorname{Sht}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),\nu}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}}
are small v-stack.

Proof.

See the proof of
[
FS21
, Proposition III.1.3]
.

∎

3.
The Geometric Satake

We follow the same notations as in the previous section. Moreover, we define the derived category of a small v-stack
X
with
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-coefficient to be

D^{b}_{\text{ét}}(X,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}):=\underset{E}{\operatorname{colim}}\ D^{b}_{\text{ét}}(X,E),

where
E
ranges over subfields of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
that are finite extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
,
D^{b}_{\text{ét}}(X,E)

is the idempotent completion of
D^{b}_{\text{ét}}(X,\mathcal{O}_{E})[\frac{1}{l}]

and the colimit is taken in the
\infty
-categorical sense. Recall that the derived category
D^{b}_{\text{ét}}(X,\mathcal{O}_{E})
with adic coefficients is defined in
[
Sch17
, Section 26]
. The six functors formalism generalizes directly to this setting.

Remark 3.1
.

The constructions in this paper work already with the coefficient ring
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(\sqrt{q})}
, and we can work with that instead.

Remark 3.2
.

The definition given here compares favorably with the constructible derived categories of schemes

4
4

4

Indeed, it follows from
[
HRS23
, Section 7.2]
that for a qcqs scheme
X
,

D_{c}^{b}(X,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})\cong\underset{E}{\operatorname{colim}}D_{c}^{b}(X,E)
,

D_{c}^{b}(X,E)
is equivalent to the idempotent completion of
D_{c}^{b}(X,\mathcal{O}_{E})[\frac{1}{l}]

and
D_{c}^{b}(X,\mathcal{O}_{E})\cong\underset{n}{\operatorname{lim}}D_{c}^{b}(X,\mathcal{O}_{E}/l^{n})
.

, see
[
BS13
, Section 6.8]
or
[
HRS23
]
, which suffices for our purpose in this paper. Moreover, the comparison functor from schemes to the associated diamonds is fully faithful, see
[
Sch17
, Proposition 27.2]
and the appendix.

Let us recall the geometric Satake proved by Fargues–Scholze.

Theorem 3.3
.

(Fargues–Scholze)
Let
I
be a finite set, and
\mu_{\bullet}
be a collection of dominant geometric cocharacters of
G
indexed by
I
. Let
\{I_{1},\cdots,I_{k}\}
be a partition of
I
. There is a natural functor

U\longmapsto\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

from the category of representations of
\hat{G}^{I}
, the product of the dual group of
G
(with
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-coefficient), to the category
D_{\text{ét}}^{b}(\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
. It has the following properties.

1) If
U
is irreducible with highest weight
\mu_{\bullet}
, then
\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
is supported on
\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
.

2) Let

\zeta:I\rightarrow J

be a map of finite sets,
(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})
be a partition of
J
, and
I_{i}=\zeta^{-1}J_{i}
, then there exists a canonical isomorphism

i_{\zeta,*}^{\operatorname{loc}}p_{\zeta}^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\cong\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta}^{*}U}^{\operatorname{loc},(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})},

where

i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})}

is defined in (
4
),

p_{\zeta}^{\operatorname{loc}}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}\rightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

is the projection on the first factor,
and

\Delta_{\zeta}:\hat{G}^{J}\rightarrow\hat{G}^{I}

is the map sending

(g_{j})_{j\in J}
to
(g_{\zeta(i)})_{i\in I}
.

3)
There is a canonical isomorphism

(\pi^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})})_{!}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\cong\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)},

where

\pi^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}

is the convolution map defined in (
5
).

4) Let
(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})
be a partition of
I
such that
J_{j}=I_{i_{j}}\cup\cdots\cup I_{i_{j+1}-1}
for some partition
1=i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{l+1}=k+1
of
k+1
,
then we have a canonical isomorphism

(\kappa^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})})^{*}\underset{j}{\boxtimes}\mathscr{S}_{U_{j}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{i_{j}},\cdots,I_{i_{j+1}-1})}\cong\mathscr{S}_{\boxtimes U_{j}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},

where

\kappa^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\longrightarrow\underset{j}{\prod}\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{i_{j}},\cdots,I_{i_{j+1}-1})}

is defined in (
6
), and
U_{j}
is a representation of
\hat{G}^{J_{j}}
.

5)
We have a canonical isomorphism

r^{*}c_{X}^{*}A_{\mu_{\bullet}}\cong\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\cdots,\{n\})}

compatible with the isomorphisms in 1), 2),3) and 4),
where
r
is the natural restriction map

r:\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet},s}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\cdots,\{n\})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc}(m),\diamond\diamond}_{\mu_{\bullet}}

from the local Hecke stack to the truncated Witt vector Hecke stack

X=\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc}(m)}_{\mu_{\bullet}}

as defined in
[
XZ17
, Definition 5.1.2]
and recalled in appendix
A.3
,

U
is irreducible with highest weight
\mu_{\bullet}
,
c_{X}
is the comparison functor defined in appendix
A.1
, and
A_{\mu_{\bullet}}
is the Satake sheaf on
\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc}(m)}_{\mu_{\bullet}}
.
More precisely,
A_{\mu_{\bullet}}
is the descent of the intersection sheaf
\operatorname{IC}_{\mu_{\bullet}}
, as defined in
[
XZ17
, Section 3.4.1]
,
on the Witt vector affine Grassmannian
Gr_{\mu_{\bullet}}
,
which exists by the
L^{+}G
-equivariance of
\operatorname{IC}_{\mu_{\bullet}}
.

Proof.

It is enough to work with the coefficient of the ring of integers of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}[\sqrt{q}]
, which is treated in
[
FS21
]
.
We follow the ideas in the proof of
[
Laf18
, Theorem 1.17]
.

Let us first consider the case that
U=\underset{j}{\boxtimes}U_{j}
, where
U_{j}
is a representation of
\hat{G}^{I_{j}}
.
From the geometric Satake proved in
[
FS21
, Theorem VI.11.1]
, there exists a canonical perverse sheaf
\mathscr{S}_{V}^{\operatorname{loc},(J)}
on
\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(J)}
for each representation
V
of
\hat{G}^{J}
. Let
\{I_{1},\cdots I_{k}\}
be a partition of
I
, and we define

\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:=(\kappa_{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})})^{*}\underset{j}{\boxtimes}\mathscr{S}_{U_{j}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{j})}.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{j}
be the algebra of regular functions on
\hat{G}^{I_{j}}
, which we view as an ind-(finite-dimensional) representation of
\hat{G}^{I_{j}}
through the left regular representation, and we define

\mathscr{S}_{\underset{j}{\boxtimes}\mathcal{R}_{j}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:=\operatorname{colim}\mathscr{S}_{\underset{j}{\boxtimes}U_{j}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},

where the colimit is over all (finite-dimensional) sub-representations of
\underset{j}{\boxtimes}\mathcal{R}_{j}
which are of the form

\underset{j}{\boxtimes}U_{j}

with
U_{j}
representations of
\hat{G}^{I_{j}}
.

Now let
U
be an arbitrary (finite-dimensional) representation of
\hat{G}^{I}
, we define

\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:=(\mathscr{S}_{\underset{j}{\boxtimes}\mathcal{R}_{j}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\otimes U)^{\hat{G}^{I}},

where
\hat{G}^{I}
acts diagonally on the tensor product, and

(-)^{\hat{G}^{I}}
means taking
\hat{G}^{I}
-invariants.
The action of
\hat{G}^{I}
on

\underset{j}{\boxtimes}\mathcal{R}_{j}

is through right multiplication on
\hat{G}^{I}
, so it is an action in the category of representations of
\hat{G}^{I}
(recall that we view
\mathcal{R}_{j}
as a left regular representation), which gives rise to an action of
\hat{G}^{I}
on

\mathscr{S}_{\underset{j}{\boxtimes}\mathcal{R}_{j}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

by the functoriality of the geometric Satake equivalence established in
[
FS21
, Theorem VI.11.1]
.

Now it is enough to prove the claims in the case
U=\underset{j}{\boxtimes}U_{j}
, which we assume from now on.
Note that 4) is automatic from the definition, using the identification

\kappa_{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}=(\underset{j}{\prod}\kappa_{(I_{i_{j}},\cdots,I_{i_{j+1}-1})}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{i_{j}},\cdots,I_{i_{j+1}-1})})\circ\kappa^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}
.

By the proof of
[
FS21
, Proposition VI.9.4]
,

(\pi^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})})_{!}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

is the fusion product of
\mathscr{S}_{U_{j}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{j})}
, then
[
FS21
, Proposition VI.10.3]
tells us that the corresponding representation is
\underset{j}{\boxtimes}U_{j}=U
. In other words,

(\pi^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})})_{!}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\cong\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)},

which proves 3).

For 1), we observe that

(\kappa_{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})})^{-1}\underset{j}{\prod}\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{j})}=\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}.

It follows from the proof of
[
FS21
, Theorem VI.11.1]
that
\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}

is the intersection sheaf of
\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}
,
where
\mu
is the highest weight of
U
. In particular,
\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}
is supported on
\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}
, so the claim follows from our observation.

To prove 2), we first prove that it holds for the trivial partition
(J)
, namely

(8)

i_{\zeta,*}^{\operatorname{loc}}p_{\zeta}^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\cong\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta}^{*}U}^{\operatorname{loc},(J)}.

We observe that both sides are natural with respect to compositions of
\zeta
. More precisely,
let

\zeta_{1}:I\longrightarrow J

and

\zeta_{2}:J\longrightarrow K

be two maps between finite sets, then
we see immediately from the definition that

i_{\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc}}=i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{2}}\circ(i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{1}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J},\zeta_{2}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}).

We have the following lemma for the naturality of the left hand side of (
8
) with respect to
\zeta
.

Lemma 3.4
.

We have a canonical identification

i_{\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1},*}^{\operatorname{loc}}p_{\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\cong i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{2},*}p_{\zeta_{2}}^{\operatorname{loc},*}i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{1},*}p_{\zeta_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}.

Proof.

We first note that the projection

p_{\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc}}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}\rightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}

factorizes through

\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}\overset{q}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta_{1}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}\overset{p_{\zeta_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc}}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)},

where

q:\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta_{1}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J},\zeta_{2}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta_{1}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}

is the projection onto the first factor.

We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta_{1}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(J)}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J},\zeta_{2}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{1}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J},\zeta_{2}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\zeta_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\zeta_{2}}^{\operatorname{loc}}}

with the middle square being Cartesian.
Then we have

i_{\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1},*}^{\operatorname{loc}}p_{\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\cong i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{2},*}(i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{1}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J},\zeta_{2}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K})_{*}q^{*}p_{\zeta_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\cong i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{2},*}p_{\zeta_{2}}^{\operatorname{loc},*}i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{1},*}p_{\zeta_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)},

where the last identification is the base change isomorphism.
∎

Therefore, if we have established the identification (
8
) for both
\zeta_{1}
and
\zeta_{2}
, then

i_{\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1},*}^{\operatorname{loc}}p_{\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\cong i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{2},*}p_{\zeta_{2}}^{\operatorname{loc},*}i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{1},*}p_{\zeta_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}\cong i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta_{2},*}p_{\zeta_{2}}^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta_{1}}^{*}U}^{\operatorname{loc},(J)}\cong\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta_{2}}^{*}\Delta_{\zeta_{1}}^{*}U}^{\operatorname{loc},(J)}\cong\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1}}^{*}U}^{\operatorname{loc},(K)},

proving (
8
) for

\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1}
.

We know that
\zeta
can be written as a composition of simple degenerations and injections, i.e. a simple degeneration is a surjective map collapsing only two elements, and a simple injection is an injective map adding only one element, and we have seen that it is enough to prove (
8
) for these two cases. Moreover, they can be written as the disjoint union of (identity map on) a finite set with either
\{1,2\}\twoheadrightarrow\{1\}

or
\emptyset\hookrightarrow\{1\}
. Using
[
FS21
, Proposition VI.10.3]
, we can further reduce to the case that
\zeta
is either
\{1,2\}\twoheadrightarrow\{1\}

or
\emptyset\hookrightarrow\{1\}
.

We check
\{1,2\}\twoheadrightarrow\{1\}
first. In this case,
i_{\zeta}^{\operatorname{loc}}
is an isomorphism and
p_{\zeta}^{\operatorname{loc}}
is the map

p^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1,2\})}

identifying
\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}
with the substack of
\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1,2\})}
where the two legs are the same. We can assume that
U=U_{1}\boxtimes U_{2}
is decomposable, then there is a canonical identification

p_{\zeta}^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1,2\})}\cong\mathscr{S}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}_{\Delta_{\zeta}^{*}U}

where
\Delta_{\zeta}:\hat{G}\hookrightarrow\hat{G}^{2}

is the diagonal map and
U
is a representation of
\hat{G}^{2}
. This is because
\Delta_{\zeta}^{*}U=U_{1}\otimes U_{2}
and

p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1,2\})}
is the convolution product of
\mathscr{S}_{U_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}

and
\mathscr{S}_{U_{2}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{2\})}
(using
\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1,2\})}
is the fusion product of
\mathscr{S}_{U_{1}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}

and
\mathscr{S}_{U_{2}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{2\})}
), see the paragraph following the proof of
[
FS21
, Proposition VI.9.4]
. Then the identification follows from the geometric Satake (
[
FS21
, Theorem VI.11.1]
).

Next, we assume that
\zeta
is
\emptyset\hookrightarrow\{1\}
, then
p_{\zeta}^{\operatorname{loc}}
is

p^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta}:\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\rightarrow\ast,

the unique map to the final object,
and

i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta}:\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}

is the section of the structure map corresponding to the trivial modification, i.e. the modification is an isomorphism. Then

i_{\zeta,*}^{\operatorname{loc}}p^{\operatorname{loc},*}_{\zeta}\mathscr{S}_{1}^{\operatorname{loc},(\emptyset)}\cong\mathscr{S}_{1}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}

where
1
is the trivial representation (of either
\hat{G}
or the trivial group
\hat{G}^{\emptyset}
). This follows from
\mathscr{S}_{1}^{\operatorname{loc},(\emptyset)}

being the constant local system, and
\mathscr{S}_{1}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}
being the constant local system supported on
\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}_{0}
(by 1)). The general
U
are direct sums of
1
, so the identification follows.

Now for a general partition
(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})
, we have a diagram with Cartesian squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{j}{\prod}\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{j}{\prod}\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{j})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I_{j}},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{j}{\prod}\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(J_{j})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\zeta}^{\operatorname{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\operatorname{loc},(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})}_{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod i_{\zeta}^{\operatorname{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod p_{\zeta}^{\operatorname{loc}}}

and we can pullback the identification for trivial partition to obtain the desired one.

For the comparison with Witt vector Hecke stacks. We note that when
I=\{1\}
, it follows from the paragraph before
[
Sch17
, Proposition VI.7.5]
and the proof of
loc.cit.
that under the inclusion

\operatorname{Perv}(\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu,s}^{\operatorname{loc},{(\{1\})}},\Lambda)\subset D_{\text{ét}}(Gr_{\mu}^{W,\diamond\diamond},\Lambda)
with
\Lambda=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(\sqrt{q})}
(recall that
\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu,s}^{\operatorname{loc},{(\{1\})}}=L^{+}G\setminus Gr_{\mu}^{W,\diamond\diamond}
),

c_{X}^{*}IC_{\mu}=\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}

where
X=Gr_{\mu}^{W}
is the Witt vector affine Grassmannian. Now we work with the general
IC_{\mu_{\bullet}}
, recall that the construction of
IC_{\mu_{\bullet}}
is by taking the twisted product of
IC_{\mu_{i}}
on
Gr_{\mu_{\bullet}}=\overset{\sim}{\boxtimes}\ Gr_{\mu_{i}}
,
see
[
XZ17
, Section 3.4.1]
. By passing to Hecke stacks, this corresponds precisely to

(\kappa_{(\{1\},\cdots,\{n\}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\cdots,\{n\})})^{*}\underset{j}{\boxtimes}\mathscr{S}_{U_{j}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}
,
which is the definition of
\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
.

∎

We also have the geometric Satake for global Hecke stacks.

Theorem 3.5
.

(Fargues–Scholze) Let
I
be a finite set, and
\mu_{\bullet}
be a collection of dominant geometric cocharacters of
G
indexed by
I
. Let
\{I_{1},\cdots,I_{k}\}
be a partition of
I
. There is a natural functor

U\longmapsto\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

from the category of representations of
\hat{G}^{I}
, the product of the dual group of
G
(with
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-coefficient), to the category
D_{\text{ét}}^{b}(\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
. It has the following properties.

1) If
U
is irreducible with highest weight
\mu_{\bullet}
, then
\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
is supported on
\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
.

2) Let

\zeta:I\rightarrow J

be a map of finite sets,
(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})
be a partition of
J
, and
I_{i}=\zeta^{-1}J_{i}
, then there exists a canonical isomorphism

i_{\zeta,*}p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\cong\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta}^{*}U}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})},

where

i_{\zeta}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{k})}

is defined in (
1
),

p_{\zeta}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}\rightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

is the projection onto the first factor,
and

\Delta_{\zeta}:\hat{G}^{J}\rightarrow\hat{G}^{I}

is the map sending

(g_{j})_{j\in J}
to
(g_{\zeta(i)})_{i\in I}
.

3)
There is a canonical isomorphism

(\pi^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})})_{!}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\cong\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I)},

where

\pi^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I)}

is the convolution map defined in (
2
).

4) Let
(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})
be a partition of
I
such that
J_{j}=I_{i_{j}}\cup\cdots\cup I_{i_{j+1}-1}
for some partition
1=i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{l+1}=k+1
of
k+1
,
then we have a canonical isomorphism

(\kappa^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})})^{*}\underset{j}{\boxtimes}\mathscr{S}_{U_{j}}^{(I_{i_{j}},\cdots,I_{i_{j+1}-1})}\cong\mathscr{S}_{\boxtimes U_{j}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},

where

\kappa^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\longrightarrow\underset{j}{\prod}\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{i_{j}},\cdots,I_{i_{j+1}-1})}

is defined in (
3
), and
U_{j}
is a representation of
\hat{G}^{J_{j}}
.

5) Let
U
be irreducible with highest weight
\mu_{\bullet}
, then the pullback of

\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\},\cdots,\{n\})}

restricted on
\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet},s}^{(\{1\},\cdots,\{n\})}

through the canonical map

\operatorname{Hecke}^{W}_{\mu_{\bullet}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet},s}^{(\{1\},\cdots,\{n\})}

is identified with the analytification of
A_{\mu_{\bullet}}
as defined in
theorem
3.3
(5).

More precisely, as in appendix
A.1
, we have the complex of sheaves
a_{X}^{*}c_{X}^{*}A_{\mu_{\bullet}}
on
X=\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc}(m)}_{\mu_{\bullet}}
, which can be further viewed as a complex of sheaves on
\operatorname{Hecke}^{W}_{\mu_{\bullet}}
through the identification in appendix
A.3
, and this is canonically identified with the pullback of
\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\},\cdots,\{n\})}

along

\operatorname{Hecke}^{W}_{\mu_{\bullet}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet},s}^{(\{1\},\cdots,\{n\})}.

6)
There is a natural restriction map

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},

along which the pullback of

\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

is identified with
\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
. Moreover, the isomorphisms in 1) to 5) are compatible under this restriction map with the corresponding isomorphisms in theorem
3.3
for local Hecke stacks.

Remark 3.6
.

We can state 3) for more general partitions on the target, at the cost of further complicating the notation. For example, we can consider the partial convolution map

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}

sending

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{3}}

to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{4}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{3}}

The special case as stated above suffices for our purpose.

Remark 3.7
.

We can refine the theorem using Langlands
L
-groups, see
[
FS21
, Theorem VI.0.2]
. More precisely, let
\operatorname{Hecke}_{\operatorname{Spd}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{I}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
be the Hecke stack over
\operatorname{Spd}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{I}
, see remark
2.2
. Then there is a functor from representations
U
of the L-group
(^{L}G)^{I}
to complexes of sheaves
\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
on
\operatorname{Hecke}_{\operatorname{Spd}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{I}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
with similar properties as in the theorem, which refines the functor in the theorem.

Proof.

We define
\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
as the pullback of
\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

along the canonical map

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

from global Hecke stacks to local Hecke stacks, then (1), (2), (3), (4), and (6) follow immediately from the local version in theorem
3.3
.

For (5), we note that the composition of the canonical maps

\operatorname{Hecke}^{W}_{\mu_{\bullet}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet},s}^{(\{1\},\cdots,\{n\})}\overset{r}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet},s}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\cdots,\{n\})}

is exactly the comparison map

a_{X}:\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc}(\infty),\diamond}_{\mu_{\bullet}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc}(\infty),\diamond\diamond}_{\mu_{\bullet}}

in appendix
A.1
with
X=\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc}(\infty)}_{\mu_{\bullet}}
, and the claim follows readily from theorem
3.3
(5) and the identifications in appendix
A.3
.

∎

Now we study morhpisms between the Satake sheaves
\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}
. We aim to interpret the morphisms as cohomological correspondences, similar to
[
XZ17
, Corollary 3.4.4]
. The possibility of having multiple legs makes the situation more complicated.

Let
U
be a representation of
\hat{G}^{I}
, we denote

\operatorname{Hecke}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:=\underset{\mu_{\bullet}}{\cup}\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},

where the index
\mu_{\bullet}
ranges over the highest weights of irreducible summands of
U
. Similarly for
\operatorname{Hecke}_{U|U^{\prime}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),0}
.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
I,J,K
be finite sets, and let
\{I_{1},\cdots,I_{k}\}
(resp.
\{J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}\}
) be a partition of
I
(resp.
J
). Let
\zeta:I\longrightarrow K
and
\zeta^{\prime}:J\longrightarrow K
be two maps.

Let
U
(resp.
U^{\prime}
) be a representation of
\hat{G}^{I}
(resp.
\hat{G}^{J}
), then there is a natural map

\operatorname{Hom}_{\hat{G}^{K}}(\Delta_{\zeta}^{*}U,\Delta_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}U^{\prime})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Corr}_{X}(p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},p_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})})

where
X
is the space

X:=\operatorname{Hecke}_{U|U^{\prime}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),0}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I}\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J},\zeta\times\zeta^{\prime}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K},

and the right hand side denotes the set of cohomological correspondences from

(\operatorname{Hecke}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K},p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})})

to

(\operatorname{Hecke}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J},\zeta^{\prime}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K},p_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})})

supported on

X
.

Let us first explain where the correspondence

X

comes from. The idea is that
we want to complete the correspondence diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{U|U^{\prime}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}}

to a cartesian square.

Observe that
\operatorname{Hecke}_{U|U^{\prime}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),0}

lies over

\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I}\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}
,
and we want to use
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
to pass to the same labeling set
K
, namely we base change along

\zeta\times\zeta^{\prime}:\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I}\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J}

to obtain the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J},\zeta^{\prime}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{U}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}

Now there is a natural way to complete the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J},\zeta^{\prime}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{U}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J)}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J},\zeta^{\prime}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{U}^{(I)}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{(K)},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\zeta^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\zeta}}

and it is easy to see that it gives rise to a Cartesian square

(9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{J},\zeta^{\prime}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{U}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{(K)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\zeta^{\prime}}\circ(\pi^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}\times\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\zeta}\circ(\pi^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times\operatorname{id})}

Proof.

We work on the derived level, and write
p^{!}
and
p_{!}
instead of
Rf^{!}
and
Rf_{!}
.
From the Cartesian square
(
9
),
we compute

\operatorname{Hom}(p_{1}^{*}p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},p_{2}^{!}p_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})})=\operatorname{Hom}(p_{2,!}p_{1}^{*}p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},p_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(p_{2,*}p_{1}^{*}p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},p_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}((\pi^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}\times\operatorname{id})^{*}i^{*}_{\zeta^{\prime}}i_{\zeta,*}(\pi^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times\operatorname{id})_{*}p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},p_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(i_{\zeta,*}(\pi^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\times\operatorname{id})_{*}p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},i_{\zeta^{\prime},*}(\pi^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}\times\operatorname{id})_{*}p_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(i_{\zeta,*}p_{\zeta}^{*}\pi^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}_{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},i_{\zeta^{\prime},*}p_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}\pi^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}_{*}\mathscr{S}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(i_{\zeta,*}p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I)},i_{\zeta^{\prime},*}p_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta}^{*}U}^{(K)},\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}U^{\prime}}^{(K)})

where the second identity holds because
p_{2}
is proper, the third and the fifth identity are by proper base change, the second to last identity is 3) of theorem
3.5
and the last identity is 2) of theorem
3.5
. Now geometric Satake (
[
FS21
, Theorem VI.11.1]
) tells us that

\operatorname{Hom}_{\hat{G}^{K}}(\Delta_{\zeta}^{*}U,\Delta_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}U^{\prime})\cong\operatorname{Hom}(\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta}^{*}U}^{\operatorname{loc},(K)},\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}U^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{loc},(K)}),

which pulls back to morphisms in

\operatorname{Hom}(\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta}^{*}U}^{(K)},\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}U^{\prime}}^{(K)})
, giving rise to the desired map

\operatorname{Hom}_{\hat{G}^{K}}(\Delta_{\zeta}^{*}U,\Delta_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}U^{\prime})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta}^{*}U}^{(K)},\mathscr{S}_{\Delta_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}U^{\prime}}^{(K)})=\operatorname{Hom}(p_{1}^{*}p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},p_{2}^{!}p_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U^{\prime}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}).

∎

We document some special cases of the proposition, which are variants of the constructions in
[
Laf18
, chapter 5]
.

Example 3.9
.

Let
I=J
with the trivial partition
(I)
,
K=\{1\}
, and
\zeta=\zeta^{\prime}
be the unique map from
I
to
K
. Let
U
and
U^{\prime}
be irreducible with highest weights
\mu_{\bullet}
and
\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}
, then the proposition says that

(10)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\hat{G}}(U,U^{\prime}):=\operatorname{Hom}_{\hat{G}}(\Delta^{*}_{\zeta}U,\Delta^{*}_{\zeta}U^{\prime})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{Hecke}_{U|U^{\prime}}^{(I)|(I),0}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I}\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{I},\zeta\times\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})}(p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(I)},p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U^{\prime}}^{(I)}).

If we further restrict the legs at the characteristic
p
untilt, then the support becomes

\operatorname{Hecke}_{\Delta^{*}U|\Delta^{*}U^{\prime},s}^{(\{1\})|(\{1\}),0}
, and this is a version of
[
XZ17
, Corollary 3.4.4]
.

More precisely, the
\diamond\diamond
-analytification of
loc.cit.
is the version of (
10
) for local Hecke stacks (with fixed legs), and (
10
) is the pullback of the local version along the global to local map

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I)}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{\operatorname{loc},(I)}
.

Example 3.10
.

(creation correspondences)
Let
I=\{1,2\}
with partition
(\{1\},\{2\})
,
J=\{1,2,3\}
with partition
(\{1,2\},\{3\})
,
K=\{1,2\}
,
\zeta=\operatorname{id}
, and
\zeta^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\{1,3\}\rightarrow\{1\}\\
\{2\}\rightarrow\{2\}\end{cases}
. Let
U
and
V
be representations of
\hat{G}
, and we consider

X=\operatorname{Hecke}_{1\boxtimes U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1\},\{2\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{2}\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3},\zeta\times\zeta^{\prime}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{2},

which parametrizes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{3}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}

More precisely, it parametrizes 3 legs labeled by 1,2 and 3 with the legs labeled by 1 and 2 being the same.
\varphi^{\prime}
is a modification at the legs labeled by 1 and 2, with the modification at the leg labeled by 1 being trivial, and the leg labeled by 2 bounded by
U
.
\varphi_{1}
is a modification at the two legs labeled by 1 and 2 with the one labeled by 1
being bounded by
V^{*}
and the other bounded by
U
, and
\varphi_{2}
is a modification at the leg labeled by 3 bounded by
V
.

The proposition then tells us that the canonical map

\sharp\boxtimes\operatorname{id}:1\boxtimes U\longrightarrow(V^{*}\otimes V)\boxtimes U

gives rise to a cohomological correspondence

\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}^{++}:(\operatorname{Hecke}_{1\boxtimes U}^{(\{1\},\{2\})},\mathscr{S}_{1\boxtimes U}^{(\{1\},\{2\})})\longrightarrow(\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3},\zeta^{\prime}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{2},p_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})})

supported on

X
, where

\sharp:1\rightarrow V^{*}\otimes V
is the canonical map sending 1 to the identity map in
V^{*}\otimes V\cong\operatorname{Hom}(V,V)
.

In the application below, we will take a further base change along

s\times\operatorname{id}:\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{2}

where
s:\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})

is the section corresponding to the characteristic
p
untilt. Then we obtain

(11)

\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}^{+}:(\operatorname{Hecke}_{U}^{(\{1\})},\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\})})\longrightarrow(\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})},\mathscr{S}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}),

supported on

\operatorname{Hecke}_{1\boxtimes U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1\},\{2\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{2}\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3},(\zeta\times\zeta^{\prime})\circ(s\times\operatorname{id})}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),

where we abuse the notation by not distinguishing

\mathscr{S}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}
, as a sheaf on
\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}
,
with its pullback to
\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3},\zeta^{\prime}\circ(s\times\operatorname{id})}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
. We hope the context will make it clear which support the sheaf lives on. Moreover, we have identified

\operatorname{Hecke}_{1\boxtimes U}^{(\{1\},\{2\})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{2},(s\times\operatorname{id})}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})

with

\operatorname{Hecke}_{U}^{(\{1\})}
, which is clear.

Example 3.11
.

(annihilation correspondences)
Let
I=\{1,2,3\}
with partition
(\{1\},\{2,3\})
,
J=\{1,2\}
with partition
(\{1\},\{2\})
,
K=\{1,2\}
,
\zeta=\begin{cases}\{1,2\}\rightarrow\{1\}\\
\{3\}\rightarrow\{2\}\end{cases}
, and
\zeta^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
. Let
U
and
V
be representations of
\hat{G}
, and we consider

X=\operatorname{Hecke}_{V\boxtimes V^{*}\boxtimes U|1\boxtimes U}^{(\{1\},\{2,3\})|(\{1\},\{2\}),0}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3}\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{2},\zeta\times\zeta^{\prime}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{2}.

The proposition then tells us that the canonical map

\flat\boxtimes\operatorname{id}:(V\otimes V^{*})\boxtimes U\longrightarrow 1\boxtimes U

gives rise to a cohomological correspondence

\mathscr{C}_{\flat}^{++}:(\operatorname{Hecke}_{V\boxtimes V^{*}\boxtimes U}^{(\{1\},\{2,3\})}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3},\zeta}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{2},p_{\zeta}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{V\boxtimes V^{*}\boxtimes U}^{(\{1\},\{2,3\})})\longrightarrow(\operatorname{Hecke}_{1\boxtimes U}^{(\{1\},\{2\})},\mathscr{S}_{1\boxtimes U}^{(\{1\},\{2\})})

supported on

X
, where

\flat:V\otimes V^{*}\rightarrow 1
is the evaluation map.

In the application below, we will take a further base change along

s\times\operatorname{id}:\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{2}

where
s:\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})

is the section corresponding to the characteristic
p
untilt, so we obtain

(12)

\mathscr{C}_{\flat}^{+}:(\operatorname{Hecke}_{V\boxtimes V^{*}\boxtimes U}^{(\{1\},\{2,3\})}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})},\mathscr{S}_{V\boxtimes V^{*}\boxtimes U}^{(\{1\},\{2,3\})})\longrightarrow(\operatorname{Hecke}_{U}^{(\{1\})},\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\})})

supported on

The proposition then tells us that the canonical map

In the application below, we will take a further base change along

The construction in the last example can be performed on local Hecke stacks. Note that in the local case, it is important that we restrict to legs at the characteristic
p

Proof.

As in the proof of proposition

, we have the Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V,s\times s}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\{2\}),0}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

where

is the unique map. We can compute

5
5

5

We have used that
f_{!}\mathscr{S}=f_{*}\mathscr{S}
with
f=i,i_{\zeta},i^{\operatorname{loc}}
and
\mathscr{S}
perverse in the computation and the rest of the paper, this needs explanation since
f
as such is not a closed immersion between Hecke stacks, see the footnote of
[
FS21
, page 227]
. However, the corresponding map on affine Grassmannians are closed immersion, and we obtain the desired equality by fully faithfulness of the pullback functor from perverse sheaves on Hecke stacks to equivariant perverse sheaves on affine Grassmannians. The author would like to thank an anonymous referee to point this out.

\operatorname{Hom}(\pi^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{1}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})},i^{\operatorname{loc},!}\mathscr{S}_{V\boxtimes V^{*}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\{2\})})=\operatorname{Hom}(i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{!}\pi^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{1}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})},\mathscr{S}_{V\boxtimes V^{*}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\{2\})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(i^{\operatorname{loc}}_{*}\pi^{\operatorname{loc},*}\mathscr{S}_{1}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})},\mathscr{S}_{V\boxtimes V^{*}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\{2\})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(\pi^{(\{1\},\{2\}),*}i_{\zeta}^{*}i_{*}\mathscr{S}_{1}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})},\mathscr{S}_{V\boxtimes V^{*}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\{2\})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(i_{*}\mathscr{S}_{1}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})},i_{\zeta,*}\pi^{(\{1\},\{2\})}_{*}\mathscr{S}_{V\boxtimes V^{*}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\{2\})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(i_{*}\mathscr{S}_{1}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})},i_{\zeta,*}\mathscr{S}_{V\boxtimes V^{*}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1,2\})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(\mathscr{S}_{1}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})},i_{\zeta,*}\mathscr{S}_{V\otimes V^{*}}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\hat{G}}(1,V\otimes V^{*})

where the fourth equality is the base change isomorphism, the sixth equality is 3) of theorem

, the second to last equality is 1) and 2) of theorem

, and the last equality is the geometric Satake (

, and (2)

. Indeed, it is a special case of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{1\boxtimes U,s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V,s\times s}^{(\{1\},\{2\}),0}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{1,s}^{(\{1\})}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1,2,3\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V,s\times s}^{(\{1\},\{2\})}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V,s\times s}^{(\{1,2\})}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{(\{1\})}}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),0}_{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{(\{1\},\{2\})}_{(\{1\},\{2\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}_{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{(\{1\},\{2\})}\times\operatorname{id}}

where
f
is the separating morphism
sending

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}

such that
\varphi=\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1}
, where

\varphi_{1}
is the modification at the characteristic
p
untilt, and
\varphi_{2}
is the modification at the characteristic 0 untilt.
Now the lemma follows readily from 4) of theorem
3.5
and the construction of
\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}^{+,0}
and
\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}^{+}
.
∎

4.
The Hecke Operators

In this section, we introduce the Hecke operators. We follow the same notations as above.

There is a canonical map

\epsilon_{\mu}^{(\{1\})}:\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu,\eta}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu,\eta}

that sends a shtuka
\{\mathcal{P},\varphi_{\mathcal{P}},S^{\sharp}\}

to

\{\mathcal{P},\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P},\varphi_{\mathcal{P}},S^{\sharp}\}
,
and we can use it to pullback the canonical sheaves in geometric Satake to sheaves on moduli spaces of shtukas.

Let
U
be an irreducible representation of
\hat{G}
with highest weight
\mu
. We have a diagram

(21)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu,\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu,\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu,\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu,\eta}^{\operatorname{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\mu}^{(\{1\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\mu}^{(\{1\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

where the last vertical map is the canonical restriction map. The square in the diagram is not commutative, but they become commutative after composing with the vertical map. Indeed,
recall from notation
2.16
, the legs of shtukas in the diagram are in characteristic zero, while the modification bounded by
\nu
takes place at characteristic
p
, hence it does not affect the local type of
\varphi_{\mathcal{P}}
, i.e. the diagram is commutative after composing with the vertical map.

Now we have a cohomological correspondence
\mathscr{C}_{\nu}
from
(\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu,\eta},\epsilon_{\mu}^{(\{1\}),*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\})})
to itself supported on
\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}
, namely

(22)

\mathscr{C}_{\nu}:p_{1}^{*}\epsilon_{\mu}^{(\{1\}),*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\})}\cong p_{1}^{*}\epsilon_{\mu}^{(\{1\}),*}r^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}\cong p_{2}^{*}\epsilon_{\mu}^{(\{1\}),*}r^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}\cong p_{2}^{*}\epsilon_{\mu}^{(\{1\}),*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\})}\cong p_{2}^{!}\epsilon_{\mu}^{(\{1\}),*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\})},

where we use
r^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}=\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\})}
(see 6) of theorem
3.5
), and the étaleness of
p_{2}
(proposition
2.18
) for the last isomorphism.

Now let
V
be a representation of
\hat{G}
, and

h_{V}:=\operatorname{Sat}^{\operatorname{cl}}([V])\in C_{c}(G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})//\mathcal{G}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})

be the function corresponding to
V
through the classical Satake equivalence

\operatorname{Sat}^{\operatorname{cl}}:R(\hat{G})\longrightarrow C_{c}(G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})//\mathcal{G}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})

as defined in
[
XZ17
, Section 3.5.2]
, where
R(\hat{G}):=K_{0}(\operatorname{Rep}(\hat{G}))
is the representation ring of
\hat{G}
, and
C_{c}(G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})//\mathcal{G}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
is the space of compactly supported, i.e. supported on a finite subset,
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-valued functions on the double quotient set
\mathcal{G}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\setminus G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})/\mathcal{G}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
. Note that we use that
G
is unramified here, i.e.
G_{\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
is split, so the Langlands dual group of
G_{\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
can be reduced to the dual group
\hat{G}
. We know that

C_{c}(G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})//\mathcal{G}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})

has a basis indexed by dominant cocharacters
\nu
(
\mathcal{G}
is reductive over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
), i.e.

1_{\mathcal{G}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\nu(p)\mathcal{G}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})}
’s
form a basis of

C_{c}(G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})//\mathcal{G}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
, so
C_{c}(G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})//\mathcal{G}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})=C_{c}(G(K)//\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{K}),\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
with
K
a finite unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
over which
G
splits.
Let
\Gamma_{V}
be the union of
\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}

(as closed subspaces of
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}
)
with
\nu
appearing in
h_{V}
. Then we define the Hecke operator
T_{V}
as the cohomological correspondence supported on
\Gamma_{V}
, and is
h_{V}(\nu(p))
times the cohomological correspondence (
22
) on
\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}
. More precisely, we define

T_{V}:=\underset{h_{V}(\nu(p))\neq 0}{\sum}h_{V}(\nu(p))\cdot\mathscr{C}_{\nu}

as a cohomological correspondence from

(\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu,\eta},\epsilon_{\mu}^{*}\mathscr{S}_{U})

to itself supported on

\Gamma_{V}:=\underset{h_{V}(\nu(p))\neq 0}{\cup}\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}.

Note that when
V
is irreducible with highest weight
\delta
, then

h_{V}(\nu(p))\neq 0

precisely when
\nu\leq\delta
, hence
\Gamma_{V}=\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}
. Since
\operatorname{Sht}^{\nu}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}\subset\operatorname{Sht}^{\delta}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}
as a closed substack, we can view
\mathscr{C}_{\nu}
as being supported on
\operatorname{Sht}^{\delta}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}
, whence the sum in the definition of
T_{V}
makes sense. In general
\Gamma_{V}
is the union of
\operatorname{Sht}^{\delta}_{\mu|\mu,\eta}
where
\delta
ranges over highest weights of irreducible summands of
V
.

We observe that when
\mu
is minuscule,
\mathscr{S}_{U}=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}[d]
is the shifted constant sheaf with
d
being the dimension of the Schubert variety with respect to
\mu
, and the cohomological correspondence
\mathscr{C}_{\nu}
is the canonical isomorphism

p_{1}^{*}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}[d]\cong\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}[d]\cong p_{2}^{*}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}[d]\cong p_{2}^{!}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}[d].

We assume from now on that
V
is a representation of
{}^{L}G
, which, by the proof of
[
Zhu15
, Lemma A.3]
, is equivalent to a representation of
\hat{G}
that we still denote by
V
, together with an isomorphism to its Frobenius twist
{}^{\sigma}V
, where
{}^{\sigma}V
is the representation of
\hat{G}
obtained by twisting the action of
G
on
V
with the inverse Frobenius action on
\hat{G}
, see
[
XZ17
, Section 3.4.5]
. Then the (unramified) classical Satake equivalence

\operatorname{Sat}^{\operatorname{cl}}:R(^{L}G)\longrightarrow C_{c}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})//\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})),\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})

defined in
[
XZ17
, Section 3.5.2]

provides us with
h_{V}:=\operatorname{Sat}^{\operatorname{cl}}([V])\in C_{c}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})//\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})),\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
.

5.
The excursion operators

We now start to define the excursion operators. We follow the same notations as in section
4
. In particular, let
U
be an irreducible representation of
\hat{G}
with highest weight
\mu
, and
V
be a representation of
{}^{L}G
, viewed as a representation of
\hat{G}
together with an isomorphism
{}^{\sigma}V\cong V
with its Frobenius twist
{}^{\sigma}V
.

We have a canonical map

\epsilon_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}

which is simply viewing
\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}_{1}
as
\mathcal{P}_{k+1}
. Similarly we have

\epsilon_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}:\operatorname{Sht}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),0}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),0}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}},

which forms a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),0}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),0}_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}}^{(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\mu_{\bullet}|\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\mu^{\prime}_{\bullet}}^{(I)}}

with the two squares Cartesian.

Let
W
be a representation of
\hat{G}^{I}
, we denote

\operatorname{Sht}_{W}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})}:=\underset{\mu_{\bullet}}{\cup}\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})},

where the index
\mu_{\bullet}
ranges over the highest weights of irreducible summands of
W
. Similarly for
\operatorname{Sht}_{W|W^{\prime}}^{(I_{1},\cdots,I_{k})|(J_{1},\cdots,J_{l}),0}
, and the Witt vector version.

5.1.
The creation correspondences

First we define the creation correspondences.

Let
\zeta:\{1\}\rightarrow\{1,2,3\}

be the map defined by
\zeta(1)=2
, we define

(23)

\operatorname{Sht}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,-|s\times-\times s}^{(\{1\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}\subset\operatorname{Sht}^{(\{1\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3},\zeta\times\operatorname{id}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3}

as the closed v-substack parametrizing modifications

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}_{1}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

with the first line in
\operatorname{Sht}_{U}
and the second line in

\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times-\times s}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}:=\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})}.

Moreover, the legs indexed by
U
in both the upper and lower modifications are the same.

Similarly, we define

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(\{1\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,-|s\times-\times s}\subset\operatorname{Hecke}^{(\{1\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3},\zeta\times\operatorname{id}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3}

as the v-substack parametrizing modifications

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{3}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

with the first line in
\operatorname{Hecke}_{U}^{(\{1\})}
and the second line in

\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times-\times s}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}:=\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}|_{\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}})}.

Moreover, the legs indexed by
U
in both the upper and lower modifications are the same.

We view

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(\{1\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,-|s\times-\times s}
,

\operatorname{Sht}_{\mu|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times-\times s}^{(\{1\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}

and

\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times-\times s}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}

as stacks over
\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
, and
we have a commutative diagram over
\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})

(24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,-|s\times-\times s}^{(\{1\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{U}^{(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times-\times s}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}^{(\{1\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,-|s\times-\times s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{U}^{(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times-\times s}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{U}^{(\{1\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}}

Now since the squares in diagram (
24
) are Cartesian, we can pullback the cohomological correspondence

\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}^{+}:(\operatorname{Hecke}_{U}^{(\{1\})},\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\})})\longrightarrow(\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times-\times s}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})},\mathscr{S}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})})

supported on

\operatorname{Hecke}^{(\{1\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}

defined in example
3.10
(
11
)
to obtain the desired creation correspondence

(25)

\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}:(\operatorname{Sht}_{U}^{(\{1\})},\epsilon_{U}^{(\{1\}),*}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\})})\longrightarrow(\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times-\times s}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})},\epsilon_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\}),*}\mathscr{S}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})})

supported on

\operatorname{Sht}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,-|s\times-\times s}^{(\{1\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}
.

Remark 5.1
.

The reason we use
\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times-\times s}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}

instead of
\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times-\times s}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}

is explained in the paragraph before
[
XZ17
, Remark 6.1.10]
, namely, we want the corresponding (integral) excursion operators to have the same support as the Hecke operators, so we can pullback the cohomological correspondences to Shimura varieties.

5.1.1.
The generic fiber

We now want to identify the generic fiber of the creation correspondences.

We first observe that

\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}

is identified with the generic fiber of

\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times\eta\times s}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}
, and similarly for
\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

There are natural identifications

\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times\eta\times s}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}\cong\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}

and

\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times\eta\times s}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}\cong\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}.

Proof.

When the two legs of

\mathcal{P}_{1}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{2}

are different, it is the same as the data of

\mathcal{P}_{1}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{3}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{2}

where the first modification takes places at the characteristic
p
untilt, and the second over characteristic 0 untilts. Now as the two legs of

\mathcal{P}_{3}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{2}\dashrightarrow\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}_{1}

are different, and by Beauville–-Laszlo it is the same as the data of

\mathcal{P}_{3}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime}\dashrightarrow\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}_{1}

where the first modification takes place at the characteristic
p
untilt, and the second takes place at characteristic zero untilts. Similarly for

\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,s\times\eta\times s}^{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}
.
∎

Let
\zeta:\{1\}\rightarrow\{1,2,3\}
be the map defined by
\zeta(1)=3
, and

(26)

\operatorname{Sht}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,-|s\times s\times-,\eta}^{(\{1\})|(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),0}\subset\operatorname{Sht}^{(\{1\})|(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),0}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\times\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3},\zeta\times\operatorname{id}}\operatorname{Spd}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3}

be the v-substack parametrizing modifications

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}_{1}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

with the first line in
\operatorname{Sht}_{U,\eta}
and the second line in

\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}
.
Moreover, the legs indexed by
U
in both the upper and lower modifications are the same.
In other words, it is the generic fiber of

\operatorname{Sht}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes U\boxtimes V,-|s\times-\times s}^{(\{1\})|(\{1,2\},\{3\}),0}

under the identification in lemma
5.2
.

By our convention,
\operatorname{Sht}_{U,\eta}
has its leg in characteristic zero, while
the first two modifications of the second line
take place at characteristic
p
, hence the third modification takes place at the same leg as the first line, and
the diagram is equivalent to

(27)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}

where the first two dashed arrows are modifications at the characteristic
p
untilt bounded by
V^{*}
and
V
respectively whose composition is an isomorphism, and the last dashed arrow is a modification at characteristic zero untilts bounded by
U
.
It means that

\operatorname{Sht}_{U|V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,-|s\times s\times-,\eta}^{(\{1\})|(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),0}

is a closed v-substack

\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),0}

of

\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}
,
which parametrizes diagrams as in (
27
) without the isomorphism condition, and the correspondence diagram becomes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{1\boxtimes U,s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}

where
p_{2}
is the closed immersion,
p_{1}
sends (

) to the first row

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Frob}_{S}^{*}\mathcal{P}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{3}}

The restriction of
\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}

to the generic fiber then gives rise to the cohomological correspondence

(28)

\mathscr{C}_{\sharp,\eta}:(\operatorname{Sht}_{U,\eta}^{(\{1\})},\epsilon^{(\{1\}),*}_{U}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\})})\longrightarrow(\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})},\epsilon^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),*}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U}\mathscr{S}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})})

supported on

\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),0}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

\mathscr{C}_{\sharp,\eta}

is the pullback of

\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}^{\operatorname{loc},+,0}\boxtimes\operatorname{id}

defined in lemma
3.13
(1)
along the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{1\boxtimes U,s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{1\boxtimes U,s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V,s\times s}^{(\{1\},\{2\}),0}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{1,s}^{(\{1\})}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V,s\times s}^{(\{1\},\{2\})}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V,s\times s}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\{2\}),0}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{1,s}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V,s\times s}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\{2\})}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),0}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{(\{1\},\{2\})}_{1\boxtimes U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),0}_{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{(\{1\},\{2\})}_{(\{1\},\{2\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}_{(\{1,2\},\{3\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\operatorname{loc}}\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\operatorname{loc}}\times\operatorname{id}}

where the maps between the last two rows are the global to local restriction maps (
\times\operatorname{id}
).

Proof.

Clearly the squares are Cartesian, so we can pullback cohomological correspondences. By definition of
\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}
, the restriction of
\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}

to the generic fiber,

\mathscr{C}_{\sharp,\eta}:(\operatorname{Sht}_{U,\eta}^{(\{1\})},\epsilon^{(\{1\}),*}_{U}\mathscr{S}_{U}^{(\{1\})})\longrightarrow(\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})},\epsilon^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),*}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U}\mathscr{S}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}),

is the pullback of

\mathscr{C}_{\sharp,\eta}^{+}

defined in lemma
3.14

along the
first two rows.

By lemma
3.14
,
\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}^{+}
is the pullback
of

\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}^{+,0}\boxtimes\operatorname{id}

along the middle two rows in the diagram.

Further, we know from lemma
3.13
(2) that
\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}^{+,0}

is the pullback of
\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}^{\operatorname{loc},+,0}

along the global to local restriction maps, thus
\mathscr{C}_{\sharp,\eta}

is the pullback of
\mathscr{C}_{\sharp}^{\operatorname{loc},+,0}\boxtimes\operatorname{id}
all the way from the bottom of the diagram.
∎

The key observation for us is that the above diagram factorizes through moduli spaces of Witt vector shtukas.

Lemma 5.4
.

We have the following commutative diagram whose composition is the same as that of the diagram in lemma
5.3
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}),0}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V\boxtimes U,s\times s\times\eta}^{(\{1\},\{2\},\{3\})}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{1}^{\operatorname{loc},W}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V}^{\operatorname{loc},W}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V,s\times s}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\{2\}),0}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{\mu,\eta}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{1,s}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hecke}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V,s\times s}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\},\{2\})}\times\operatorname{Hecke}_{U,\eta}^{\operatorname{loc},(\{1\})}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{\operatorname{loc},W,0}_{V^{*}\boxtimes V}\times\operatorname{id}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\operatorname{loc}}\times\operatorname{id}}

where the vertical maps along the first lines are restriction to the moduli spaces of local shtukas at the special fiber.

More precisely,

in the diagram is a shtuka version of

, namely sending

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{3}}

to

On the other hand, the map

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}

Proof.

Obvious.
∎

Similar to the

in (

Proposition 5.5
.

The creation cohomological correspondence
